===============================================================================================
CASE #33: MARIA VIRGINIA V. REMO V. THE HONORABLE SECRETARY OF FOREIGN AFFAIRS
Citation: G.R. No. 169202 | March 5, 2010 | 614 SCRA 281 | Second Division | Ponente: J. Carpio
===============================================================================================

-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LAYER A: ALAC DIGEST, RECITATION SCRIPT & STATCON ANALYSIS
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

I. PARTIES & PROCEDURAL BACKGROUND
* Plaintiff/Petitioner: Maria Virginia V. Remo (Petitioner)
* Defendant/Respondent: The Honorable Secretary of Foreign Affairs (Respondent)
* Originating Court: Office of the President / Court of Appeals
* Nature of the Action: Petition for Review on Certiorari under Rule 45 concerning a married woman's right to revert to her maiden name in her Philippine Passport under Republic Act No. 8239 (Philippine Passport Act of 1996) and Article 370 of the Civil Code.

II. WHOLE FACTS
Maria Virginia Remo, a married Filipino woman, applied to renew her Philippine passport and requested to revert to her maiden name ('Remo') while her marriage was still fully subsisting. The Department of Foreign Affairs (DFA) denied her request, citing Section 5(d) of Republic Act No. 8239 (Philippine Passport Act of 1996) and DFA Implementing Rules, which allow a married woman to revert to her maiden name in a passport ONLY upon death of the husband, annulment/divorce, or legal separation. Remo petitioned the courts, arguing that under Article 370 of the Civil Code, a married woman is not obligated to use her husband's surname and may choose to use her maiden name at any time.

Plain Meaning Summary of Facts:
A married woman who used her husband's last name on her old passport wanted to switch back to her maiden last name on her new passport, even though they were still happily married. The DFA refused, citing the Passport Act rules which say once you choose to put your husband's surname on your passport, you can only change it back if your husband dies or you get an annulment/divorce. The Supreme Court ruled that while the Civil Code allows a woman to use her maiden name in daily life, the Passport Act is a special law governing travel documents that restricts mid-marriage name changes on passports to prevent identity confusion.

III. THE LEGAL ISSUE
Whether or not a married woman who previously used her husband's surname on her passport can revert to her maiden name in passport renewal while the marriage is still subsisting.

[HOW TO TACKLE IN RECITATION: THE LEGAL ISSUE]
* Plain Meaning: Can a married woman switch back to her maiden name on her passport while still married, despite the specific restrictions in the Passport Act?
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the issue is whether Article 370 of the Civil Code overrides the special name-change restrictions under Section 5(d) of the Philippine Passport Act (RA 8239), testing the canon Generalia Specialibus Non Derogant (general laws do not derogate from special laws)."

IV. ALAC DIGEST

[A] ANSWER:
NO. A married woman who has chosen to adopt her husband's surname in her passport CANNOT revert to her maiden name while the marriage is subsisting. Under Generalia Specialibus Non Derogant, the special Passport Act prevails over the general Civil Code.

* Plain Meaning: No. Under the Civil Code, you can choose to keep your maiden name when you get married. But once you elect to put your married name on your official passport, the special Passport Act applies: you cannot switch back and forth until the marriage ends, in order to maintain international travel security.
* Recitation Script: "No, Sir/Ma'am. The Supreme Court held that while Article 370 of the Civil Code gives a married woman the option to use her maiden name, under Generalia specialibus non derogant (general laws yield to special laws), RA 8239 is a special law governing international travel documents. Once she opts to use her husband's surname in her passport, she can only revert to her maiden name upon death, divorce, or annulment."

[L] LEGAL BASIS:
* 1. Section 5(d), Republic Act No. 8239 (Philippine Passport Act of 1996): Restricts amendment of passport names of married women to instances of widowhood, annulment, or divorce.
* 2. Article 370, Civil Code of the Philippines: General rule allowing married women to use maiden name or husband's surname.
* 3. Generalia Specialibus Non Derogant (general provisions do not derogate from special provisions): The specific passport security statute prevails over the general civil law on surnames.
* Plain Meaning: Section 5(d) RA 8239, Article 370 Civil Code, and Generalia Specialibus Non Derogant.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the legal basis is Section 5(d) of RA 8239 in relation to Article 370 of the Civil Code, governed by Generalia specialibus non derogant (special laws prevail over general laws)."

[A] ANALYSIS / APPLICATION:
* 1. Harmonization of Civil Code and Passport Act: Article 370 of the Civil Code grants a woman the choice of whether to adopt her husband's surname. However, once she exercises that choice and registers her married name on her official passport, RA 8239 regulates how and when that passport name may be changed.
* 2. Special Law on Travel Documents: RA 8239 is a special law designed to protect the integrity of Philippine passports and prevent identity fraud in international travel. Its specific provisions override general civil provisions.
* 3. Validity of Administrative Regulations: The DFA regulations strictly tracking Section 5(d) of RA 8239 were valid exercises of subordinate rule-making power consistent with statutory intent.
* Plain Meaning: The Civil Code gives you the freedom to choose your name. But a passport is an international border-control document. If people could switch names back and forth on their passports during marriage, it would cause chaos for international security. The specific passport law controls over the general family law.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, the Court applied Generalia Specialibus Non Derogant. The Civil Code is general law on personal names, while the Passport Act is special law governing international travel integrity. The Court held that statutory passport restrictions control during the subsistence of the marriage."

[C] CONCLUSION:
The Supreme Court DENIED the petition and AFFIRMED the decision of the Court of Appeals and DFA denying the reversion to maiden name on the passport.

* Plain Meaning: The petition was denied and the woman was not allowed to switch her passport name while married.
* Recitation Script: "The Supreme Court affirmed the DFA's denial, upholding the primacy of the special Passport Act over general Civil Code provisions."

V. RELATION TO STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION
* Syllabus Topic Alignment:
  IV. Aids to Construction -> C. Presumptions -> c. Presumption Against Implied Repeals & Harmonization of Statutes (Civil Code Art. 370 vs Passport Act RA 8239)

* Governing Latin Maxims with English Translations:
  1. Generalia specialibus non derogant (general things do not derogate from special things)
     - Literal Translation: General words do not derogate from special provisions.
     - StatCon Application: The special provisions of the Philippine Passport Act prevail over the general provisions of the Civil Code on names.
  2. Verba legis non est recedendum (from the words of the statute there should be no departure)
     - Literal Translation: From the words of the statute there must be no departure.
     - StatCon Application: Section 5(d) of RA 8239 explicitly enumerates the exclusive grounds for changing passport names.
  3. Optima statuti interpretatrix est ipsum statutum (the best interpreter of a statute is the statute itself)
     - Literal Translation: The best interpreter of a statute is the statute itself.
     - StatCon Application: The clear text of RA 8239 establishes international security standards for travel documents.

* Case Violation in StatCon: Petitioner sought to elevate general civil law provisions over a specific, subsequent statute governing international travel document security.
* Doctrinal Execution: Primacy of Special Regulatory Statutes: Special statutes enacted to protect state security and international document integrity override general civil law provisions on the same subject.

* Plain StatCon Meaning: A special security law on passports beats a general civil law about family names.
* Recitation Script: "Sir/Ma'am, Remo v. Secretary of Foreign Affairs is the leading modern case applying Generalia specialibus non derogant to resolve conflicts between the Civil Code and special regulatory statutes."

* J. Statutory Construction vs. Judicial Legislation - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court underscored the constitutional boundary of judicial power: Courts are empowered solely to interpret (jus dicere), not to enact or amend laws (jus dare). Supplying unwritten terms or expanding clear prohibitions constitutes prohibited judicial legislation.
* K. Legis Interpretatio Legis Vim Obtinet - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. Under Article 8 of the Civil Code, the Supreme Court's authoritative interpretation of the statute forms part of the legal system of the Philippines and acquires the force of law (*legis interpretatio legis vim obtinet*).
* L. Extent and Limits of the Power to Construe - [APPLICABLE]: Applicable. The Court respected the constitutional limits of its authority by applying the law as written and refusing to invade the legislative domain under the guise of equity or expediency.

VIII. OTHER CANONS OR METHODS OF CONSTRUCTION
* Canon/Method: • Verba legis non est recedendum (clear statutory text on passport name changes)
• Optima statuti interpretatrix est ipsum statutum (statute is its own best interpreter)
* How Applied: Applied as corroborative canons to reinforce the primary rule of construction.
* Effect on Interpretation: Harmonized the statute and prevented an absurd or unjust result.

IX. THE CONSTRUCTION MADE BY THE COURT
* Summary Formula: The Court construed the disputed statutory provisions in Maria Virginia V. Remo v. The Honorable Secretary of Foreign Affairs to mean and apply strictly in accordance with Generalia specialibus non derogant, holding that No. Under the Civil Code, you can choose to keep your maiden name when you get married. But once you elect to put your married name on your official passport, t.
* Principal Reasoning: The Civil Code gives you the freedom to choose your name. But a passport is an international border-control document. If people could switch names back and forth on their passports during marriage, it would cause chaos for international security. The specific passport law controls over the general family law.

X. STATUTORY CONSTRUCTION OR JUDICIAL LEGISLATION?
[X] Proper statutory construction    [ ] Judicial legislation    [ ] Debatable
Explanation: The Supreme Court acted within the legitimate bounds of its constitutional power to interpret the law (jus dicere). By applying Generalia specialibus non derogant, the Court faithfully effectuated the legislative intent without creating new rules, modifying clear statutory text, or usurping congressional prerogative. The ruling reinforces the separation of powers and the doctrine that courts must enforce the law as enacted.

XI. DOCTRINE:
Primacy of Special Regulatory Statutes: Special statutes enacted to protect state security and international document integrity override general civil law provisions on the same subject.

XII. ONE-SENTENCE TAKEAWAY:
This case is important in Statutory Construction because A special security law on passports beats a general civil law about family names.
